[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 11051)\nTotal output lines: 3\n\n{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"09592e","wall_time_seconds":0.3685753,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1068,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: Read-aloud lecture archive\\r\\ncreated: 2026-09-30\\r\\nupdated: 2026-09-30\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\ntags: [lsat, lectures, teleprompter]\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Lectures ready to teach\\r\\n\\r\\nEach lecture package keeps the source transcript, read-aloud script, visual timeline, and board/question captures together. The script retains the source\'s instructional sequence and teaching detail while removing names, attendance chatter, and class logistics. Spoken words and unspoken display cues are separate.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Choose a section\\r\\n\\r\\n| Section | Archive |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| Logical Reasoning | [Topics, core lectures, and advanced lectures](logical-reasoning/index.md) |\\r\\n| Reading Comprehension | [Topics and lecture levels](reading-comprehension/index.md) |\\r\\n\\r\\nConcept tags: contrapositives, linking conditional rules, applying facts to rules, illegal reversal, illegal negation, AND/OR, quantifier scope, inference pivots, numerical boundaries, requirement language, and passive voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find a lecture by concept\\r\\n\\r\\n| Search phrase | Tag | Lecture preparation |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Applying facts to rules | `fact-triggers-rule` | [Before teaching: Core Must Be True](logical-reasoning/must-be-true/core/2026-08-27-33013/teaching-brief.md) |\\r\\n| Linking rules | `conditional-linking` | [Before teaching: Core Must Be True](logical-reasoning/must-be-true/core/2026-08-27-33013/teaching-brief.md) |\\r\\n| Inference family | `inference-family` | [Before teaching: Core Must Be True](logical-reasoning/must-be-true/core/2026-08-27-33013/teaching-brief.md) |\\r\\n| Conditional logic | `conditional-logic` | [Before teaching: Core Must Be True](logical-reasoning/must-be-true/core/2026-08-27-33013/teaching-brief.md) |\\r\\n| Contraposition | `contrapositive` | [Before teaching: Core Must Be True](logical-reasoning/must-be-true/core/2026-08-27-33013/teaching-brief.md) |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Call up a lecture during preparation\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk: \\"Open the Core Must Be True lecture,\\" \\"Use the lecture on applying facts to rules,\\" or \\"Find the lecture on linking rules.\\" Follow the concept links above for preparation, then open the lecture\'s `teleprompter.html`. The files are saved in this second brain; the localhost URL is a viewing convenience and may need its local server restarted after a reboot.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teleprompter is a private teacher guide. Read SAY and ASK aloud. WAIT tells you when to pause; EXPECTED RESPONSE gives private answer guidance and likely wrong turns. BOARD and QUESTION copy only the material you need for the whiteboard. Next up previews the following moment, and Read ahead lets you inspect the upcoming sequence. Advance manually when the student is ready. The continuous script remains available when you want to read the whole lesson before teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Files kept with each lecture\\r\\n\\r\\n`<logical-reasoning-or-reading-comprehension>/<topic>/<core-or-advanced>/<lecture-date>-<video-id>/` holds:\\r\\n\\r\\n- `transcript.md`: faithful timestamped source text, with raw evidence and hash references.\\r\\n- teleprompter.html: private teacher view with short spoken passages, Socratic guidance, previews, and copy controls.\\r\\n- lesson-steps.json: ordered teacher cues, questions, expected reasoning, likely mistakes, and follow-up prompts.\\r\\n- teleprompter.md and full-script.html: continuous reading script with images and unspoken cues.\\r\\n- `screenshots/`: initial teaching board, every worked question, and important annotated board states.\\r\\n- `timeline.json`: source ranges, concepts, question identifiers when verified, script segments, and visual links.\\r\\n- `coverage.md`: full-lecture concept/question coverage, intentional cuts, and unresolved gaps.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal captures are preserved separately under `raw/sources/lsat-lab-lectures/<video-id>/` with provenance and hashes. Corrections do not overwrite source evidence. No lecture is marked complete until the full transcript and every worked question have been checked.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach folder level has an `index.md`. Topics may be question types, question families, or reasoning skills. A lecture has one canonical home; overlapping topics use links and tags. Core/advanced describes its archive placement, not the difficulty of every question inside it. Platform and instructor remain lecture metadata rather than an extra routing layer.\\r\\n"}}\n{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"c551fc","wall_time_seconds":0.3805759,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":14207,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 14207)\\nTotal output lines: 628\\n\\n---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn\'t tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn\'t answered that question.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas\\r\\n\\r\\nAn [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Measure in that subset | Observation |\\r\\n|---|---:|\\r\\n| Prose paragraphs | 73 |\\r\\n| Prose words | 2,743 |\\r\\n| Approximate sentences | 223 |\\r\\n| Median sentence length | 10 words |\\r\\n| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |\\r\\n| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |\\r\\n| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |\\r\\n| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |\\r\\n| Median paragraph length | 33 words |\\r\\n| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |\\r\\n| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source uses contractions throughout: I\'m, you\'re, we\'ve, we\'ll, there\'s, doesn\'t, don\'t, can\'t, couldn\'t, isn\'t, and what\'s. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You don\'t know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Punctuation follows the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nUse full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source\'s occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That\'s it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Word choice and useful verbal habits\\r\\n\\r\\n### Ordinary language carries the formal idea\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what\'s left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |\\r\\n| Possibility | might, could, may, we don\'t know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |\\r\\n| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that…7207 tokens truncated…ual contraposition follows both condition roles and families, and precedes notation. Chains, Groups 3/4 and quantities still receive later development. The source does not contain those complete later curricula.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Accuracy and method boundaries\\r\\n\\r\\nWriting that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua\'s chosen method.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |\\r\\n| Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |\\r\\n…1051 tokens truncated…\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Read it back: if someone edits this newsletter, they have an account. That matches the sentence.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Try the reverse. If someone has an account, do they necessarily edit the newsletter? The sentence hasn\'t told us that. An account could belong to someone who only reads it.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe final possible counterexample is introduced as something consistent with the sentence, not an asserted fact about this system. Technical placement has a semantic reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Give a flashcard a reasoning task\\r\\n\\r\\n**Front**\\r\\n\\r\\n> Every package in Locker 4 has been scanned. A package has been scanned. Must it be in Locker 4? Why?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Back**\\r\\n\\r\\n> No. The rule tells us about packages already in Locker 4. It doesn\'t say every scanned package goes there.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is more useful than a card that only asks “Necessary condition = which side?” Keep simple recall cards when recall is the actual skill; do not mistake that for complete reasoning practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Make feedback identify the missed connection\\r\\n\\r\\n> You used the sentence in the reverse direction. “Only registered visitors enter the archive” requires registration for entry. It hasn\'t said every registered visitor enters. Read the diagram as **Enters → Registered** and compare it with the sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe feedback names the mistake, shows the relevant meaning, and gives a repair. It avoids generic praise, mockery, and labels such as “weak reasoning.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### Explain an ordinary learning objection\\r\\n\\r\\n> “I understood the sentence. Why write it down?”\\r\\n>\\r\\n> With one short rule, you may not need to. When a passage gives you several relationships, a diagram lets you compare them without rereading the wording each time. In this exercise, write the relationship so we can check that you kept its direction and complete ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe answer gives a decision criterion and distinguishes the purpose of this exercise from a blanket rule about every test question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Transfer the voice to another LSAT topic\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conclusion is the claim the author is asking you to accept. The other statements are supposed to support it.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Nora says, “The library should stay open later because every evening seat was taken this week.” What is she trying to persuade us of?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That the library should stay open later. The full seats are her reason. She expects that reason to support the proposed change.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You don\'t have to agree with her. First, separate what she wants you to accept from what she gives you in support.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe same mechanism works outside conditionals: definition, small example, question, explanation, and a useful boundary. No conditional topic has been imported into the conclusion lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Revise the draft in a useful order\\r\\n\\r\\n### First, check the reasoning and scope\\r\\n\\r\\nList what is given, what follows, and why. Try to construct a case that satisfies the given information but makes the proposed conclusion false. If that case is possible, revise the logic before polishing the voice. Check modality, negation, quantifier scope, and whether the learner has actually been taught every required operation.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a rewrite, preserve Joshua\'s established method and explicit lesson scope. The new reference governs delivery. It does not authorize removing content that students need or adding source topics the current assignment does not cover.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, check the learner\'s experience\\r\\n\\r\\nRead as someone who has never seen the notation. Can they tell what the concept is? Can they answer the first question with what the page has taught? Does each new term refer to an idea they have seen? Does the page tell them why the correct answer follows?\\r\\n\\r\\nPut the explanation beside the point where it is needed. Do not rely on an interaction to imply a distinction the prose never explains. A student who cannot use the animation must still be able to learn from the text.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Then, listen to the voice\\r\\n\\r\\nRead aloud at an ordinary teaching pace. Replace stiff phrasing with the words the teacher would naturally use. Keep the intermediate thoughts. Vary sentence length where the task changes. Remove repeated catchphrases, inflated enthusiasm, branded metaphors, and irrelevant asides.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not repair generic prose by scattering “Cool,” “Let\'s,” or “That\'s it” through it. Repair the missing reasoning, the abrupt transition, or the abstract example.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Finally, verify the student surface\\r\\n\\r\\nCheck the actual prompt, answer state, labels, buttons, diagrams, and explanations. A good note can be undermined by a shell that exposes internal planning or reveals the answer too soon. Preview desktop and mobile when the interface changes. Keep the instructor\'s planning map separate from what the learner sees.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing\\r\\n\\r\\nScore each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Dimension | What a strong draft does |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |\\r\\n| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |\\r\\n| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |\\r\\n| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |\\r\\n| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |\\r\\n| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |\\r\\n| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |\\r\\n| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |\\r\\n| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |\\r\\n| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |\\r\\n| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |\\r\\n| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua\'s response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.\\r\\n\\r\\n## A reusable writing brief\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this when giving another writer or agent the task. Supply the actual source, lesson scope, prerequisite knowledge, and approved method along with it.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nWrite this LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice.\\r\\nRead wiki/teaching-voice.md, references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md,\\r\\nand wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md before drafting.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep the reader beside the teacher. Start with the ordinary meaning of\\r\\nthe idea, then make it concrete with an original, intuitive example.\\r\\nShow each small inference in natural language. Ask the question a learner\\r\\nwould actually need to answer here. When the question is practice, leave\\r\\nroom for an attempt before revealing the reasoning.\\r\\n\\r\\nVary sentence length by purpose. Let explanations breathe and use short\\r\\nsentences to settle a consequence or a limit. Use contractions naturally.\\r\\nTeach the technical term when there is a clear idea to attach it to.\\r\\nRepeat an anchor only when the repetition changes the learner\'s view.\\r\\nShow a tempting mistake and how to detect it when that distinction matters.\\r\\n\\r\\nPreserve Joshua\'s method, the exact logical strength, complete conditions,\\r\\nand the selected topic scope. Give fresh practice with explained answers.\\r\\nDo not copy the reference\'s examples, jokes, personal claims, or procedural\\r\\ncounts. No em dashes, hype, invented evidence, internal stage language,\\r\\nor decorative catchphrases. Light humor is optional and must help the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the result aloud, check every inference, and review the student-facing\\r\\nprompt and answer states. If the reader cannot hear why the next thought\\r\\nfollows, revise the explanation before polishing the surface.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Keep this reference current\\r\\n\\r\\nThis file is the detailed method reference, not a second canonical brand voice. [Teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md) remains the router. [COPY-CORRECTIONS](../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md) remains the record of demonstrated wording failures. Add Joshua\'s later calibration to the relevant section here rather than creating competing LSAT voice documents.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe project-owned `lsat-u-voice` skill reads this standard. Recap and bootcamp workflows also read it explicitly. An older vendor voice skill describes a different writer blend; it must not override this Joshua-selected standard in this project. Managed plugin caches are preserved rather than silently rewritten.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source archive is immutable. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"87db26","wall_time_seconds":0.3608129,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3340,"output":"---\\r\\ntitle: Teaching Voice\\r\\ntype: concept\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\\r\\ncreated: 2026-06-10\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nstatus: active\\r\\nsubject: Teaching Voice\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Teaching Voice\\r\\n\\r\\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**LSAT teaching standard, selected 2026-10-02:** Joshua chose the supplied Conditional Reasoning transcription as the reference for how LSAT material should sound and unfold. Read [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) before drafting lessons, notes, explanations, drills, flashcards, quizzes, or instructional feedback. It contains the detailed source analysis, original calibration examples, progression, and review rubric. [Source identity and limits](sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) are preserved separately. This choice develops the teaching-content register below; correspondence and marketing retain their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core voice (every register)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\r\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\r\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\r\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\r\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Air between ideas. Real correspondence is short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters). In teaching, vary sentence and paragraph length with the reasoning: give an explanation room, then let a short sentence settle the consequence. Do not impose email brevity on a lesson or cut its intermediate reasoning.\\r\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\"; \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\r\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\r\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\r\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\r\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\r\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs: never.)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The apology / accountability move\\r\\n\\r\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Hi Sandra,\\r\\n>\\r\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\r\\n> [link]\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Best,\\r\\n> Joshua\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\r\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\r\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\r\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\r\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\r\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\r\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\r\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\r\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Situational micro-templates\\r\\n\\r\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\r\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\r\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\r\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\r\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availability plainly, no overselling, hand them the next step.\\r\\n  > Thanks for reaching out ... I\'d be happy to talk more about what you\'re working on and see if we\'d be a good fit. You can schedule a consultation here.\\r\\n- **Student under real-life stress:** meet the person first.\\r\\n  > Please don\'t apologize ... it sounds like you are dealing with an incredibly stressful and demanding situation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The three registers\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich voice governs which surface.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Coaching (earnest) -- inbox, personal encouragement, email\\r\\n\\r\\nFully earnest. Warm, sincere, calm, patient. No jokes. The closest thing to being in the room with Joshua. Normalizes struggle, then moves to the work.\\r\\n\\r\\nReference line (target tone):\\r\\n\\r\\n> Totally normal to trip on this one. Let\'s slow down: what is the author actually concluding? Once you see it, the trap answers lose their grip. You\'ve got this.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Encouragement (student missed a hard one):** honest first, then forward. Name that it\'s genuinely hard, stay calm, move to the work. Not soft-reassuring, not cold.\\r\\n  > Missing this is expected. It\'s one of the hardest moves on the test. Let\'s slow down and see where it slipped.\\r\\n- **Accountability (student isn\'t doing the work):** gentle nudge, low pressure. Frame it as a slip, find a rhythm together. Never \\"the reps aren\'t happening,\\" never tough love.\\r\\n- **Praise (student nails it):** name the exact skill, tie it to their history, raise the bar.\\r\\n  > That\'s the cleanest you\'ve split premise from conclusion yet. Let\'s push it into the harder sets.\\r\\n- **Hard truth about the test:** state it plainly. \\"Do not expect for it to come easy. Expect to find this exhausting.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. Teaching content (conversational reasoning) -- lessons, recaps, notes, drills, explanations\\r\\n\\r\\nUse [LSAT teaching voice standard](../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md), Joshua\'s selected calibration from 2026-10-02. Keep the reader beside the teacher. Explain the ordinary meaning, ground it in an original example, make the small inferences audible, ask a useful question, and show both the consequence and its limit. Introduce terminology and notation when their meaning is established. Give each concept enough development to be learned from the page.\\r\\n\\r\\nContractions and varied sentence lengths should sound natural when read aloud. Repetition should change the reader\'s view of the same relationship. Questions should give the reader actual thinking work. Light humor may appear within an explanation when it helps understanding; this supersedes the older wit-only-at-the-edges restriction. Do not force punchlines, slang, hyperbole, catchphrases, or paragraph quotas.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source governs delivery, not Joshua\'s procedure, indicator taxonomy, biography, or the scope of a lesson. Preserve his approved methods and logical precision. Personal encouragement still follows the earnest coaching register. Project-owned `lsat-u-voice` routes here and to the detailed standard; an older vendor writer-blend skill must not override this selection.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\\r\\n\\r\\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed \\"the next card is the page in front of you\\" was clever but empty).\\r\\n\\r\\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\\r\\n\\r\\nBrand copy speaks as the platform (\\"we\\", \\"you\\"), not as an individual (\\"I\\", \\"Joshua\\"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Don\'t\\r\\n\\r\\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\\r\\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\r\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\r\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\r\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\r\\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\\r\\n"}}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"status":"fulfilled","value":{"chunk_id":"423591","wall_time_seconds":0.2896048,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":560,"output":"31:Joshua\'s marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. [Conditional Logic | LR](https://app.lsatlab.com/video-library/?videoid=16784), Patrick Tyrrell, published 2020-07-28. Observed Vimeo embed: 438082783. Parent verified player runtime 24:34. Caption source: `raw/sources/lsat-lab-lectures/16784/source.en-US.vtt`, native en-US track. No audio playback or board inspection is claimed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source summary\\r\\n\\r\\nThe lesson begins with rule meaning and four possible fact positions, establishing valid triggering and invalid reversal/negation. Contraposition expands immediately to compound conditions. Chaining then contrasts rule conclusions with factual conclusions, including a partly satisfied compound trigger. Translation follows these operations: certainty, requirements, indicator direction, negative universals, exception language, and biconditionals. Training uses rhetorical inference questions, two explicit early pause tasks, equivalent translations, and a ten-item pause-and-solve worksheet with an indicator reference. The instructor reviews each item’s indicator, diagram direction, negation, and contrapositive. Later items demand interpretation beyond the memorized list and compound negation. The closing prioritizes indicator fluency, contraposition, compound negation, and avoiding invalid moves. Automated feedback, timed practice, mastery checks, and actual LSAT question IDs are absent from the captions. Quantifiers receive a brief translation aside, not a full treatment.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Chronological factual topic ledger\\r\\n\\r\\nTimes identify approximate teaching transitions in overlapping subtitle cues. Entries are topic labels and observed activity metadata.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Time | Topic | Observed training format |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 00:10–00:31 | Objectives | Introduction |\\r\\n| 00:31–01:02 | Arrow; certainty; sufficient/necessary | Concrete rule |\\r\\n| 01:02–01:41 | Positive trigger; absent trigger | Contrasting cases |\\r\\n| 01:41–02:31 | Positive consequent; reversal; flaw | Contrast and warning |\\r\\n| 02:31–02:58 | Absent consequent | Inference question |\\r\\n| 02:58–04:05 | Contraposition; invalid negation | Explanation |\\r\\n| 04:05–04:37 | Compound antecedent; AND/OR negation | Symbolic demonstration |\\r\\n| 04:37–05:14 | Compound consequent contraposition | Explicit pause; worked answer |\\r\\n| 05:14–05:45 | Rule conclusion versus fact conclusion | Task distinction |\\r\\n| 05:45–06:37 | Three-rule chains; intermediate inferences | Worked symbolic chain |\\r\\n| 06:37–07:11 | Disjunctive outcome with eliminating fact | Worked symbolic inference |\\r\\n| 07:11–07:53 | Partly satisfied conjunction; residual conditional | Explicit pause; worked answer |\\r\\n| 07:53–09:05 | Certainty; universals; guarantees; requirements | Recognition and requirement rephrasing |\\r\\n| 09:05–09:29 | Equivalent wording | Pause to inspect slide examples |\\r\\n| 09:29–10:19 | Indicator columns; semantic categories | Reference list |\\r\\n| 10:19–11:45 | Negative universal | Plausibility contrast; alternate translations |\\r\\n| 11:45–12:53 | Negative quantifier rephrasing | Brief comparison |\\r\\n| 12:53–14:49 | Unless/until/without | Two equivalent approaches; simple examples |\\r\\n| 14:49–16:29 | Exception language with compounds | Parentheses; negation; alternate formulation |\\r\\n| 16:29–17:51 | Biconditional indicators | Two arrows versus same-status cases |\\r\\n| 17:51–18:48 | Otherwise; explicit opposite rule | Worked interpretation |\\r\\n| 18:48–19:29 | Ten-item worksheet instructions | Reference aid; pause; translation plus contraposition |\\r\\n| 19:29–19:49 | Item 1: when | Answer review |\\r\\n| 19:49–20:15 | Item 2: implies | Answer review |\\r\\n| 20:15–20:32 | Item 3: no | Answer review |\\r\\n| 20:32–20:49 | Item 4: each | Answer review |\\r\\n| 20:49–21:18 | Item 5: unless | Two approaches |\\r\\n| 21:18–21:53 | Item 6: never | Unlisted-word interpretation |\\r\\n| 21:53–22:19 | Item 7: any; alternative will reading | Scope and ambiguity |\\r\\n| 22:19–22:39 | Item 8: only | Answer review |\\r\\n| 22:39–23:18 | Item 9: without plus AND | Compound negation; alternate approach |\\r\\n| 23:18–23:42 | Item 10: prerequisite | Unlisted-word interpretation |\\r\\n| 23:42–24:20 | Fluency goals | Recap |\\r\\n| 24:20–24:35 | Outro | End material |\\r\\n\\r\\nNo proprietary worksheet sentences or answer bank are reproduced here.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Original analysis: prerequisites and learning demand\\r\\n\\r\\nThis compact lesson assumes the learner can handle abstract letters and elementary negation almost immediately. It introduces AND/OR contraposition approximately four minutes in, before detailed indicator translation. That makes it useful as a compressed overview or refresher; a ground-up independent pathway would benefit from more isolated practice before the combined tasks. This judgment concerns instructional demand, not evidence of learner outcomes.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe four fact-position cases teach an important boundary: from S→N, S licenses N and ¬N licenses ¬S; ¬S and N alone leave the other term undetermined. Students must interpret uncertainty as an answer rather than feel compelled to infer something.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe linking section requires a different output type from triggering. Rules alone yield another relationship. Adding a fact can yield a factual consequence. A fact satisfying part of an AND antecedent can also simplify the remaining conditional demand. This last operation merits its own assignable skill in LSAT U rather than being hidden under general linking.\\r\\n\\r\\nTranslation strategy is partly syntactic and partly semantic. The reference identifies arrow placement, but unlisted words and equivalent requirement readings require understanding necessity. The lesson therefore does not justify teaching indicator memorization as sufficient mastery.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe practice sequence shifts from guided cases to a mixed worksheet and delayed instructor review. This is feedback through worked explanation after the student pauses. Captions provide no evidence of answer entry, immediate adaptive feedback, a scored diagnostic, retries with fresh items, or gating. A ten-item mixed worksheet is one observed training event, not a complete inventory of LSAT Lab exercises.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Original LSAT U examples and assignable skills\\r\\n\\r\\nAll examples below are new and are not paraphrases of source exercises.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Fact positions:** every licensed pilot has passed an exam. Test separately what follows from licensed, not licensed, passed, and not passed.\\r\\n- **Compound contraposition:** inspected AND sealed → dispatchable. If not dispatchable, at least one prerequisite failed. Students must not infer both failed.\\r\\n- **Rule versus fact:** regional → registered; registered → approved. Derive regional → approved. Only after a named applicant is regional may the student derive that applicant’s approval.\\r\\n- **Residual condition:** code AND badge → entry; entry → attendance. Given the badge, code → attendance still remains conditional until a code fact is supplied.\\r\\n- **Equivalent exception translations:** equipment operates only when a safety check has passed. Require both a necessity reading and its contrapositive.\\r\\n- **Biconditional scope:** a display lights exactly when the circuit is active. Contrast an explicitly given two-way rule with an unsupported reversal of a one-way rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Joshua\'s Beginner/Medium/Advanced model, start with a single operation, then mix operations, then vary language and compounds. Use a separate general mixed assignment at each level. These are design proposals, not LSAT Lab\'s observed level definitions.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Nuances and source-fidelity limits\\r\\n\\r\\nThe short negative-quantifier aside includes few, most, and not-all transformations. In LSAT U, define the intended mathematical meaning of few before equating it with most-not; everyday usage can be vague. No existence assumption should be silently imported into a universal rule. The captions do not give a full quantifier inference lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe certainty discussion includes guaranteed causal wording. LSAT U should distinguish the logical guarantee encoded by a sentence from proof of causation in an argument. The lesson\'s broad certainty framing is a recognition heuristic, not a complete account of every kind of statement.\\r\\n\\r\\nOtherwise creates a biconditional in the discussed case because the other branch supplies the opposite outcome. It should not be taught as automatically biconditional in every sentence.\\r\\n\\r\\nNative-track labeling does not establish transcription accuracy. Captions contain obvious substitutions involving contrapositive, flaw, letters, names, and ordinary nouns. One compound explanation labels the inferred side inconsistently; diagrams are necessary to verify its intended symbols. The item with alternative any/will readings requires the unseen sentence and subject scope to adjudicate fully. The slide\'s complete indicator list, displayed diagrams, and exact written worksheet were not recovered by reading captions. Do not fill these gaps from guesses.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Coverage check\\r\\n\\r\\nAll 573 numbered cues were read from opening to final cue; the initially truncated middle was read separately. First cue: 00:02.960. Final cue: 24:33.080–24:35.140. Only gaps greater than two seconds are the initial 2.960-second lead-in and 6.030 seconds before the final outro cue. Overlapping cues are normal here. Final timestamp exceeds the observed 24:34 runtime by 1.140 seconds; preserve this mismatch rather than trimming the source.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis establishes complete coverage of the supplied caption stream and its teaching sequence. It does not establish audiovisual fidelity, complete board content, or a complete LSAT Lab curriculum.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2026-10-02 public video review and opening correction\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected the local first page: it asked what a rule tells the student before explaining what conditionals are or why they matter. This supersedes acceptance of the earlier opening preview; tests and visual checks did not establish teaching quality.\\r\\n\\r\\nReviewed Patrick\'s official public [Conditional Logic | LSAT Logical Reasoning](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jeDnykOrswY), observed runtime 24:34. Full exported auto-generated English caption stream covers 00:02 through 24:33. Read every caption. A read-only comparison found all 573 caption texts match the earlier archived lesson 16784 exactly. This verifies public-video identity with the prior caption analysis. No separate LSAT Loud title has been established; that wording is treated as a likely dictation reference to LSAT Lab, not verified product metadata.\\r\\n\\r\\nFresh browser visual inspection adds evidence beyond the prior caption-only review:\\r\\n\\r\\n- 01:05: the same NYC/USA rule has a separate Linda fact and a question before the conclusion is shown.\\r\\n- 01:10: the conclusion is revealed beside that fact; the original relationship remains visible.\\r\\n- 01:25: an absent trigger is contrasted with an unknown outcome; the board explicitly permits either outcome.\\r\\n- 02:40: a separate contrapositive board reverses and negates the two terms. This was viewed, not imported into the introductory lesson.\\r\\n- 09:04: a requirement reading and combined requirement chain are on the board.\\r\\n- 09:14: equivalent expression families are grouped on a reference board, following the explicit pause instruction at about 09:05.\\r\\n- 19:19: an indicator reference is available during the pause-and-diagram instructions before answer explanations.\\r\\n\\r\\nScreenshots are in visual-proof/ with the lab-primer prefix and timestamps. No invalid frame is offered as proof. Source screenshots are private research evidence, not student lesson assets. No full real-time audio watch or precise silence-duration measurement is claimed.\\r\\n\\r\\nExplicit pause requests from the captions occur around 04:39, 07:11, 09:05 and 19:16. Earlier rhetorical questions and staged visual reveals establish question-before-answer progression, not a verified multi-second silent pause.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe design pattern to adapt is one relationship, one changed fact, a learner-controlled thinking interval, then the explanation and a contrasting mistake. The video itself introduces abstract symbols and contraposition quickly. Joshua\'s requested first page should be slower: what a condition means; what a conditional statement connects; why this matters on the LSAT; certainty compared with usual likelihood; one fully explained original example; then a pause before applying it. Sufficient/necessary terminology and indicator families belong after this introduction. Explain what the relationship lets us know and why that matters for LSAT conclusions. Read one original rule naturally. Explain that must rules out an exception to the stated relationship. If it is absent, this rule alone does not settle the other condition. If it is present, the required condition follows. Explain both conclusions before revealing the answers. |\\n19:| **3. Show equivalent ways to express one relationship. Explain grouped forms and optional endings. Explain why understanding the sentence matters more than spotting a word. | Compare ordinary-English versions of a stable relationship; identify the complete sufficient idea. No arrow-construction task yet. Add current Group 1 expressions as clearly contextual extensions here. |\\n20:| **4. Necessary: what has to be true** | Name the then-part. Explain its required role when the sufficient condition is true. Establish certainty about that requirement. Avoid implying that the condition must happen later in time. | In the same anchor, explain what is required and why. Keep the sufficient and necessary roles visible as complete ideas. |\\n21:| **5. Show how these express the same relationship as the earlier family. Give only if special attention: it identifies a requirement despite containing if. Explain that symbols will help keep the relationship clear when wording changes. | Read and compare necessary-language sentences, including a controlled if versus only-if contrast. Add current Group 2 forms here without silently reclassifying Joshua\'s groups. |\\n22:| **6. Discover the contrapositive** | Restate the sufficient-to-necessary relationship. Use a fresh thought experiment. Remove the necessary condition and ask whether the sufficient condition could still be true. Pause. Derive the answer from the unmet requirement. Name the contrapositive only after that reasoning, then return to the anchor. | Predict before reveal, explain the contradiction in ordinary English, and apply the reasoning to the anchor. Do not start with flip-and-negate. |\\n23:| **7. Compare all four situations** | Teach in the source\'s chart order: sufficient present; necessary absent; sufficient absent; necessary present. The first establishes the necessary condition; the second rules out the sufficient condition; the other two leave the other condition unsettled by this rule alone. Explain each abbreviation. | Watch a narrated construction, then place complete ideas and read the result back in English. Sentence order can differ from diagram order. |\\n25:| **9. Write and read the contrapositive** | Diagram the meaning already learned. Introduce the not-sign. Work through all four sign patterns: neither side negative, necessary negative, sufficient negative, both negative. Derive reverse-and-negate as a shortcut. Explain equivalence. Then distinguish writing the contrapositive from reading it when useful. | Build and read each form; explain why the transformed rule says the same thing. Answer practical diagramming questions** | Keep the source\'s FAQ order: when to diagram; how to check correctness and build confidence; concern about time; when to write a contrapositive; why training still asks for written contrapositives; how to choose readable abbreviations. | Compare a diagram with the original sentence. Diagnose a specific mistranslation and record what caused it. Start untimed; timing can be added to teacher-assigned medium/advanced practice. |\\n27:| **11. Read this before\\r\\nwriting or editing copy for LSAT U so the same mistake is not made twice.\\r\\n\\r\\nCompanion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This\\r\\nsays what has actually gone wrong and why.\\r\\n\\r\\nStarted 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a\\r\\ncorrection is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The rules these corrections came from\\r\\n\\r\\nThree rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was\\r\\nenforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface\\r\\nadded later starts uncovered.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.\\r\\n- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`\\r\\n  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.\\r\\n- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nGuards now live with the content rather than with each component:\\r\\n`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every\\r\\nsurface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional opening: define the words before using them (2026-10-03)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua\'s marked browser paragraphs used “direction” and “guarantee” before making their meaning clear. The purpose paragraph also described an argument accepting more than its information supports without showing what that meant. Teach what a rule lets you conclude in plain language, then attach the vocabulary to a concrete relationship. Explain sufficient as enough and necessary as required before relying on those roles. Show what the must-arrow records and why English clause order does not determine the two sides.\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule describes what would follow **if** a condition were true; it does not say that condition happened. Bold that “if” and use meaningful in-view motion to expose the connection, with a static reduced-motion equivalent. Replace compressed ready-order wording with a natural full sentence. Give each paragraph and interaction a specific teaching job. These corrections supplement the canonical voice standard rather than adding a new writing register.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Generic reasoning prompts → concrete numbered demonstrations (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua marked four parts of the V6 opening: \\"Read each whole idea,\\" \\"Find what the statements guarantee,\\" the abstract first sentence about information, and the paragraph combining application and linking. They named goals without showing a useful action or explanation. Replace them with numbered, separate scenarios. Show the fact and rule that prove a conclusion about one case; separately show two matching rules and their new conditional conclusion. Labels should identify actual inputs or outputs. Review the whole note for repeated umbrella instructions and make task instructions executable. This follows the teaching voice: specific, concrete, short sentences, with air between distinct ideas.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Fact and rule definitions must explain the inference (2026-09-30)\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua rejected definitions that remained abstract and an explanation that did not clearly distinguish applying a rule from linking rules. Define the inputs with examples, then show the matching step and exact output. A case fact plus a rule proves something about that case. Nothing comes back out yet…\\" → plain instruction\\r\\nClinical and abstract, and the sentence then told her to read \\"with full\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"c4efab","wall_time_seconds":0.1694851,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2776,"output":"139:<h1>Conditional Reasoning</h1>\\n142:<h2>The Art of the If/Then</h2>\\n156:<h3>The Sufficient Condition</h3>\\n158:<div class=\\"callout\\">The “if” part of the conditional is called the sufficient condition.</div>\\n162:<p>The sufficient condition is a door opener. It’s welcoming. It could occur, but it doesn’t have to. Just like an open door, someone may walk in, but they don’t have to. In the example, I might be breathing or I might not: I could be holding my breath trying to get rid of hiccups or I could be breathing. We don’t know. We just know what will happen if I <em>am</em> breathing.</p>\\n164:<p><strong>If the sufficient condition is absent, you can completely ignore the conditional statement.</strong> If I’m not breathing, this conditional doesn’t matter at all. There might be air; there might not be air. The conditional wasn’t activated, so who cares? I’m not breathing, so it doesn’t matter.</p>\\n166:<p><strong>But if the sufficient condition is present, the conditional statement must be followed.</strong> If I am breathing, there absolutely 100% has to be air. The conditional statement gets activated and everything goes according to plan.</p>\\n170:  <p>It means the sufficient condition is present! So the conditional must be followed.</p>\\n173:<p>The sufficient condition extends beyond just the word “if.” There is a whole family of words that work just like “if”; they let you know you have a sufficient condition on your hands.</p>\\n175:<p>Here are the members of the sufficient family:</p>\\n191:<p>Notice how all of the sufficient family members have similar personalities. They are all inclusive, open words. Think back to the similarities between these words if/when you get lost in complex language.</p>\\n193:<p>I had to smooth out some of the linguistic rough edges to translate that conditional into all the different versions, but the core relationship remained the same. <strong>Language is the challenge of conditionals; you have to distill the same conditional relationship from many different wording combinations.</strong> This is why it’s so important to understand the relationship on a deeper level than “look for indicator words.” The words are the hard part, so relying on indicators exclusively is a ticking time bomb.</p>\\n197:  <p>The parentheses are referring to an optional addition to the original word. Both “when” and “whenever” are members of the sufficient family. Conditionally, all of the examples in the chart above mean exactly the same thing as our initial example.</p>\\n200:<h3>The Necessary Condition</h3>\\n202:<div class=\\"callout\\">The “then” part of the conditional is called the necessary condition.</div>\\n206:<p>The necessary condition slams the door that the sufficient condition opens. It is the place of certainty. I mean just look at its name! It’s called the <em>necessary</em> condition because it is literally necessary. It has to occur at some point if the conditional relationship is activated. You can prove things that live in the necessary condition.</p>\\n208:<p>There is a family of words that serves the same purpose as “then.” Each of these words lets you know you have a necessary condition in play. Here are the members of the necessary family:</p>\\n215:    <tr><td>necessary</td><td><em>Air is necessary for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\n228:<p>All of those statements in the example column mean the exact same thing as the sufficient family examples. It is the exact same concept expressed in a bunch of different ways. What’s important isn’t how the conditional is expressed, but the core relationship hiding underneath the language. This is why we have a symbolic way of representing conditional relationships, to push past the language and focus only on the relationship between the two variables.</p>\\n230:<div class=\\"note\\"><strong>Pay special attention to ONLY IF!</strong> It is a necessary indicator, even though it contains the word IF. You can think of it like the ONLY is dominant over a recessive IF.</div>\\n234:<h2>The Contrapositive</h2>\\n236:<p>So far, we’ve explored the sufficient condition, the necessary condition, and the relationship between the two. Specifically, we’ve gone over the core of the conditional relationship:</p>\\n238:<p class=\\"example\\">If the sufficient condition occurs, then the necessary condition must occur.</p>\\n254:<p>If you are not on your way to get a coffee, you haven’t met your necessary burden, right? So could you have activated your sufficient condition? NO.</p>\\n259:<p class=\\"example\\">If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive.</p>\\n261:<p>As you’ve seen, the contrapositive can be produced organically. It is not some foreign process that needs to be performed on conditionals. <strong>The contrapositive is sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized.</strong> If you have not met your necessary burden, you cannot have activated the sufficient condition.</p>\\n267:<p>OK, so if I’m breathing, air is needed. I have to have air for the breathing to happen. The air is completely necessary. What if I don’t have air? That means I don’t have the thing I need. That implies that I couldn’t have activated my conditional relationship. So, I can’t be breathing. No necessary conditions means there’s no sufficient condition.</p>\\n269:<p><strong>The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your conditional relationship.</strong> The LSAT will try to tempt you into erroneously surmising other things from your conditional. Don’t fall for these ploys. For example, they will give you a conditional statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” <strong>The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.</strong></p>\\n278:    <tr><td><strong>Sufficient Present</strong></td><td><em>Must have necessary condition</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, so there must be air.</em></td></tr>\\n279:    <tr><td><strong>Necessary Absent</strong></td><td><em>Can’t have sufficient condition</em></td><td><em>There’s no air, so I can’t breathe.</em></td></tr>\\n289:<h2>How to Diagram Conditionals</h2>\\n309:        necessary<br>\\n322:<h3>The Must Arrow</h3>\\n335:  <li>See a conditional indicator.</li>\\n337:  <li>Place an abbreviation of the word or phrase targeted by the conditional indicator on the correct side of the Must Arrow.\\n339:      <li>Sufficient is on the left, before the Must Arrow.</li>\\n340:      <li>Necessary is on the right, after the Must Arrow.</li>\\n350:<p>I see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a <strong>B</strong> in the sufficient condition:</p>\\n358:<p>There you go. Your first fully diagrammed conditional! Now let’s talk through the contrapositive. If the necessary condition is gone, we know the sufficient is gone as well. We can diagram the contrapositive as:</p>\\n390:<h3>Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ</h3>\\n397:    <p>Besides timing concerns, I mostly see people decline to diagram because they aren’t confident that their diagram will be correct, and they feel safer sticking to the exact words of the stimulus because then they “can’t screw it up.” But you need to fail a couple times in order to build the skill. That’s how you get better. Don’t give up before you start. This leads me to the next question.</p>\\n410:    <p>Also, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.</p>\\n435:<h2>Figuring Out The Language</h2>\\n437:<p>Let’s tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning: figuring out the language. We’ll start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word.</p>\\n439:<p><strong>The indicator’s target is the word or phrase it is referring to.</strong></p>\\n441:<p>In this list, I put the conditional indicator’s target in a little bracket cage.</p>\\n455:<p>Notice that in each of these examples, the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read, but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. This generalization isn’t always true:</p>\\n460:    <tr><td><em>[Air] is necessary for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\n465:<p>In these examples, the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult, you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is <strong>really saying</strong>. They don’t want you to see an indicator word and autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Many of them can switch sides. Look at the variations on a few of these conditionals:</p>\\n468:<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is necessary for breathing. / Breathing makes it necessary to have [air].</p>\\n475:<h2>Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then</h2>\\n477:<div class=\\"callout\\">Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals.</div>\\n481:<p><strong>Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation.</strong> This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world.</p>\\n483:<h3>How to Create a Personalized If/Then</h3>\\n489:  <li>Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.</li>\\n491:  <li>If your guess is right, you will know that the necessary condition has to pertain.</li>\\n498:<p>Before you proceed, think about what this sentence means for real. You need to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning before you can check your personalized if/then against that meaning. The sentence means orchids need frequent conversation. First we’ll guess that orchids is the sufficient condition and see if this fits with our casual meaning of the sentence:</p>\\n504:<p>Now, let’s imagine that I guessed incorrectly and tried to see if frequent conversation could be the sufficient condition. The personalized if/then would help me catch my error:</p>\\n506:<p>So, if I’m a frequent conversation, what do I know about me? If I’m a frequent conversation, what do I need? Do I need an orchid? Ehh… I don’t need anything, right? Orchids are the ones who need something in this sentence. If I’m frequent conversation, I don’t <em>need</em> anything else. This tells me that frequent conversation is not the correct sufficient condition. This is how you can detect that you’ve made a mistake. It is the incorrect translation of the conditional.</p>\\n512:<h2>Basic Conditional Drill — Much Ado About Nothing</h2>\\n516:<h3>Instructions</h3>\\n537:    <p>I am heart-burned an hour after whenever I see him.</p>\\n"}'}]
