[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"136aaf","wall_time_seconds":0.2733929,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1926,"output":"\\r\\n<p>Conditional reasoning is the art of the <em>if/then</em>. If one thing happens, then another thing must happen. That’s it.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s explore our first conditional relationship:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Because you’re a thinking human, you can probably guess what this means at a literal level. If I’m breathing, there’s air around. The core of the relationship is that simple. If we have one thing, we <strong>must</strong> have the other thing. Just like with the Power Players, we have to take this <strong>MUST</strong> seriously. There is no way around this relationship. If you have the first thing, you have to have the second thing.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>For all the ballyhoo about conditional reasoning, you would think it’s a lot more complicated than that. The key to keeping conditional reasoning simple through its many applications, which we will examine in detail throughout this chapter, is understanding the concept behind it. I want you to intuitively understand the relationship at the heart of conditionals. Once you have that, the verbal idiosyncrasies that separate one conditional from another will be nothing more than blips on the radar.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s start by taking our conditional example apart piece by piece.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>The Sufficient Condition</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">The “if” part of the conditional is called the sufficient condition.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The sufficient condition is a door opener. It’s welcoming. It could occur, but it doesn’t have to. Just like an open door, someone may walk in, but they don’t have to. In the example, I might be breathing or I might not: I could be holding my breath trying to get rid of hiccups or I could be breathing. We don’t know. We just know what will happen if I <em>am</em> breathing.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>If the sufficient condition is absent, you can completely ignore the conditional statement.</strong> If I’m not breathing, this conditional doesn’t matter at all. There might be air; there might not be air. The conditional wasn’t activated, so who cares? I’m not breathing, so it doesn’t matter.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>But if the sufficient condition is present, the conditional statement must be followed.</strong> If I am breathing, there absolutely 100% has to be air. The conditional statement gets activated and everything goes according to plan.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">\\r\\n  <strong>What does it mean to activate a conditional?</strong>\\r\\n  <p>It means the sufficient condition is present! So the conditional must be followed.</p>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The sufficient condition extends beyond just the word “if.” There is a whole family of words that work just like “if”; they let you know you have a sufficient condition on your hands.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here are the members of the sufficient family:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead><tr><th>Sufficient Indicators</th><th>Example</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td>if</td><td><em>If I’m breathing, then air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>when(ever)</td><td><em>When I’m breathing, air is present.</em><br><br><em>Whenever I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>any(time)</td><td><em>Any breathing requires that air is present.</em><br><br><em>Anytime I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>all</td><td><em>All instances of breathing have air present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>every(time)</td><td><em>Every instance of breathing has air present.</em><br><br><em>Everytime I’m breathing, air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>in order to</td><td><em>In order to breathe, I must have air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>people who</td><td><em>People who breathe need air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>each</td><td><em>Each instance of breathing has air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Notice how all of the sufficient family members have similar personalities. They are all inclusive, open words. Think back to the similarities between these words if/when you get lost in complex language.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>I had to smooth out some of the linguistic rough edges to translate that conditional into all the different versions, but the core relationship remained the same. <strong>Language is the challenge of conditionals; you have to distill the same conditional relationship from many different wording combinations.</strong> This is why it’s so important to understand the relationship on a deeper level than “look for indicator words.” The words are the hard part, so relying on indicators exclusively is a ticking time bomb.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">\\r\\n  <strong>What’s with all the parentheses? What does when(ever) mean?</strong>\\r\\n  <p>The parentheses are referring to an optional addition to the original word. Both “when” and “whenever” are members of the sufficient family. Conditionally, all of the examples in the chart above mean exactly the same thing as our initial example.</p>\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>The Necessary Condition</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">The “then” part of the conditional is called the necessary condition.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">If I’m breathing, then air is present.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>The necessary condition slams the door that the sufficient condition opens. It is the place of certainty. I mean just look at its name! It’s called the <em>necessary</em> condition because it is literally necessary. It has to occur at some point if the conditional relationship is activated. You can prove things that live in the necessary condition.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>There is a family of words that serves the same purpose as “then.” Each of these words lets you know you have a necessary condition in play. Here are the members of the necessary family:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <thead><tr><th>Necessary Indicators</th><th>Example</th></tr></thead>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td>then</td><td><em>If I’m breathing, then air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>must</td><td><em>Air must be present for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>necessary</td><td><em>Air is necessary for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>required</td><td><em>Air is required for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>only (if)</td><td><em>Only the presence of air allows for breathing.</em><br><br><em>I’m breathing, only if air is present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>depends</td><td><em>Breathing depends on air being present.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>need (to)</td><td><em>I need air to breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>have to</td><td><em>I have to have air, if I’m breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>essential</td><td><em>Air is essential for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td>precondition</td><td><em>Air is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Notice the similarities in each of these words. They are all ironclad words. They are serious and restrictive; they are obligations.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>All of those statements in the example column mean the exact same thing as the sufficient family examples. It is the exact same concept expressed in a bunch of different ways. What’s important isn’t how the conditional is expressed, but the core relationship hiding underneath the language. This is why we have a symbolic way of representing conditional relationships, to push past the language and focus only on the relationship between the two variables.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\"><strong>Pay special attention to ONLY IF!</strong> It is a necessary indicator, even though it contains the word IF. You can think of it like the ONLY is dominant over a recessive IF.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"91ad50","wall_time_seconds":0.2463274,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1447,"output":"<h2>Figuring Out The Language</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning: figuring out the language. We’ll start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>The indicator’s target is the word or phrase it is referring to.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>In this list, I put the conditional indicator’s target in a little bracket cage.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>If [I’m breathing], then air is present.</em></td><td><em>Each [instance of breathing] has air.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>When [I’m breathing], air is present.</em></td><td><em>I’m breathing, only if [air is present].</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>Any [breathing] means air is present.</em></td><td><em>Only [the presence of air] allows for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>All [instances of breathing] have air present.</em></td><td><em>Breathing depends on [air being present].</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>Every [instance of breathing] has air present.</em></td><td><em>I need [air] to breathe.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>In order to [breathe], I must have air.</em></td><td><em>I have to have [air], if I’m breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>People who [breathe] need air.</em></td><td></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Notice that in each of these examples, the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read, but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. This generalization isn’t always true:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<table>\\r\\n  <tbody>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] must be present for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is essential for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] is necessary for breathing.</em></td><td><em>[Air] is a precondition for breathing.</em></td></tr>\\r\\n    <tr><td><em>[Air] is required for breathing.</em></td><td></td></tr>\\r\\n  </tbody>\\r\\n</table>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>In these examples, the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult, you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is <strong>really saying</strong>. They don’t want you to see an indicator word and autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Many of them can switch sides. Look at the variations on a few of these conditionals:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] must be present for breathing. / There must be [air] for us to breathe.</p>\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is necessary for breathing. / Breathing makes it necessary to have [air].</p>\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">[Air] is required for breathing. / I require [air] to breathe.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>[Final line at the bottom of the image is partially obscured.]</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n<h2>Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then</h2>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"callout\\">Personalized if/thens are translations for conditionals.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Here’s another safeguard on conditional diagramming. <strong>Make a habit of translating conditionals into personalized if/thens.</strong></p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>Personalized if/thens require you to put yourself in the conditional statement and ask yourself what you know about your situation.</strong> This can get you out of confusion panic and back to the real world.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<h3>How to Create a Personalized If/Then</h3>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Imagine you’re confused on what a conditional means. Instead of panicking:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<ol>\\r\\n  <li>Try to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning of the sentence.</li>\\r\\n  <li>Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition.</li>\\r\\n  <li>Put yourself in your guess’ situation and ask what you know about yourself.</li>\\r\\n  <li>If your guess is right, you will know that the necessary condition has to pertain.</li>\\r\\n</ol>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Let’s translate an example into a personalized if/then:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p class=\\"example\\">Orchids depend on frequent conversation.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Before you proceed, think about what this sentence means for real. You need to understand the casual, non-conditional meaning before you can check your personalized if/then against that meaning. The sentence means orchids need frequent conversation. First we’ll guess that orchids is the sufficient condition and see if this fits with our casual meaning of the sentence:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<div class=\\"note\\">The key to intuitively understanding conditionals is personalizing them. Always put yourself in the shoes of the conditional, no matter what it’s about.</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So, if I’m an orchid, what do I know about me? I know that I have to have frequent conversation. I depend on frequent conversation. If I’m an orchid, <em>then</em> I have frequent conversation. This fits. It is the correct translation of the conditional.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>Now, let’s imagine that I guessed incorrectly and tried to see if frequent conversation could be the sufficient condition. The personalized if/then would help me catch my error:</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p>So, if I’m a frequent conversation, what do I know about me? If I’m a frequent conversation, what do I need? Do I need an orchid? Ehh… I don’t need anything, right? Orchids are the ones who need something in this sentence. If I’m frequent conversation, I don’t <em>need</em> anything else. This tells me that frequent conversation is not the correct sufficient condition. This is how you can detect that you’ve made a mistake. It is the incorrect translation of the conditional.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<p><strong>The personalized if/then is how I always do conditionals, unless they are already phrased as if/thens.</strong> It gives me confidence throughout the test that I’m not making a mistake.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n<hr class=\\"page-break\\">\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"7a5d7b","wall_time_seconds":0.2333295,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1725,"output":"### Give an idea several teachable moments\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve the real indicator groups\\r\\n\\r\\nThe current reviewed opening materials use:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\\r\\n- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\\r\\n\\r\\nSee the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source\'s two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua\'s notes and scope.\\r\\n\\r\\n“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Preserve Joshua\'s five steps\\r\\n\\r\\nIn [Sandra\'s 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua\'s five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Carry these improvements forward\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.\\r\\n2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.\\r\\n3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.\\r\\n4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.\\r\\n5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.\\r\\n6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.\\r\\n7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.\\r\\n8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.\\r\\n9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.\\r\\n10. **Keep each operation behind its prerequisites.** Under the 2026-10-03 sequence, conceptual contraposition follows both condition roles and families, and precedes notation. Chains, Groups 3/4 and quantities still receive later development. The source does not contain those complete later curricula.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Accuracy and method boundaries\\r\\n\\r\\nWriting that sounds like the source must still preserve correct logic and Joshua\'s chosen method.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Source simplification or context | Boundary for our material |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| “Ignore the conditional” when sufficient is absent or necessary is present. | For this one rule and fact, no new conclusion about the other condition follows. The rule remains available for later facts and chains. Never teach that it disappears. |\\r\\n| Necessary has to happen “at some point.” | A conditional is not necessarily a time sequence or causal claim. Prefer “has to be true if the sufficient condition is true.” |\\r\\n| The original and contrapositive are “all you know.” | The central warning is against an unsupported reverse or inverse. Equivalent formulations and other supplied rules still matter. State the immediate inference limits precisely. |\\r\\n| Required words are described as obligations. | Preserve modality. “Interviewees must bring ID” can assert an obligation, not prove compliance. Diagram the stated requirement unless the facts establish compliance. |\\r\\n| Indicator usually comes before target. | Read its full grammatical target. Targets can precede the indicator, and familiar words can serve other roles in context. |\\r\\n| Shorten conditions to one or two letters, never more than four. | Joshua\'s clear condensed labels govern. Keep negation, scope, qualifiers, and readability. A short label that loses meaning is not an improvement. |\\r\\n| Try a personalized sufficient guess. | Compare with the whole ordinary meaning and look for a counterexample. A feeling that an arrow is plausible is not enough. |\\r\\n| Original and contrapositive training. | Explain why a drill requires explicit work and when test-day practice may differ. Do not turn a training requirement into a universal performance rule. |\\r\\n| “Always” is in a necessary-family teaching list. | Establish the sentence\'s subject and relationship. It is not a universal instruction to place the next word on the right. |\\r\\n| Confidence guarantees the diagram is correct. | Confidence can grow from practice, but correctness rests on preserving meaning and valid reasoning. Provide checks. |\\r\\n| Timing and learning claims are absolute. | Treat them as the author\'s advice unless independently supported. ## assistant

Reviewed the supplied HTML and archived LSAT Lab captions/research. **I did not watch or listen to the video in this task.** No files edited.

The strongest source-backed teaching pattern is: **establish ordinary meaning, hold one relationship steady, change its wording, explain the complete idea the wording refers to, and then place that idea by its role.**

1. **Sufficient is enough, not proof that the condition occurred.**  
   Supplied HTML, **“The Sufficient Condition,” lines 156–197**: identifies the if-part, explains it can be present or absent, contrasts those cases, defines activation, then introduces equivalent sufficient-family sentences. Source family order is **if; when/whenever; any/anytime; all; every/every time; in order to; people who; each**. Its central warning is that recognizing a familiar word alone is insufficient.  
   Our correction: if sufficient is absent, the rule **does not settle the other condition from that fact alone**. Do not teach the source’s broader “ignore the conditional” shortcut.

2. **Necessary is what the first condition requires.**  
   HTML, **“The Necessary Condition,” lines 200–230**: returns to the same relationship, names the then-part, develops certainty, and then supplies the family in order: **then; must; necessary; required; only/only if; depends; need/need to; have to; essential; precondition**. The deliberate **if versus only-if** distinction deserves a worked contrast, rather than a list entry.  
   Our correction: required **for what**, under this particular rule? Necessary is neither automatically a later event nor proof of compliance with an instruction.

3. **“Target” means the complete idea referred to, not the next word.**  
   HTML, **“Figuring Out The Language,” lines 435–472**:
   - Defines target first.
   - Brackets complete targets in familiar sentences.
   - Shows targets following indicators.
   - Then shows targets preceding necessary language.
   - Finally moves **the same indicator** relative to the target without changing the relationship.
   
   Its examples demonstrate that **must, necessary, and required can occur on either side of their target**. This supports explicitly teaching both positions for those expressions, not merely adding one generic warning. Preserve qualifications such as current, prepaid, or submitted by Friday when finding the target.

4. **The meaning check should expose why a reversed interpretation fails.**  
   HTML, **“Your Friend, the Personalized If/Then,” lines 475–508**: understand ordinary meaning → propose a sufficient idea → imagine a case meeting it → ask what follows. It works through the correct reading and then a deliberately reversed reading.  
   Our correction: keep the person/object and grammatical role sensible; check against the whole sentence and an allowed counterexample. A plausible feeling is not proof.

5. **LSAT Lab explicitly pairs attached ideas with arrow roles, then emphasizes semantic requirements.**  
   Archived captions:  
   `raw/sources/lsat-lab-lectures/16784/source.en-US.vtt`
   - **09:05–09:16:** pause to compare differently worded sentences expressing the same relationship.
   - **09:28–09:49:** the **idea attached to** the indicator goes on the corresponding arrow side.
   - **09:49–10:20:** groups language by rules, universals, guarantees, and requirements; notes the list is incomplete; emphasizes understanding what is required, which goes right.
   
   The full visual keyword inventory cannot be recovered from captions. The archive’s lecture index routes only to the filed Core Must Be True lecture; this standalone primer’s raw evidence and analysis remain in the conditional curriculum research package.

The current project taxonomy is distinct from the HTML’s families: **Group 1: if, when, where, all, the only, every, any. Group 2: only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.** Preserve both inventories contextually. “The only” and “only” need separate whole-sentence teaching; **always** cannot mechanically mean “put the next words right.”

Authoritative references:

- `C:/Users/Joshu/OneDrive/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html`
- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/research-lsatlab-conditional-lesson.md`
- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md`, **13-section skeleton, rows 3, 5, 8, 11, 12**
- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`, **Give an idea several teachable moments; Preserve the real indicator groups; Preserve Joshua’s five steps; Accuracy and method boundaries**
- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md`, **Conditional opening: define the words before using them**
- `C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/wiki/teaching-voice.md`, **Teaching content**

Voice implications: make each small inference audible; use “enough,” “required for what,” “what do we know,” and “the sentence hasn’t told us”; keep contractions natural; give explanations room; ask an actual decision before revealing its reason. Avoid undefined “direction” or “guarantee,” procedural labels that replace explanations, copied catchphrases, and next-word placement shortcuts.

Write that as <strong>~Packed → ~Ready</strong>. The <strong>~</strong> sign means <strong>not</strong>.</p>\\r\\n      <p>To write a contrapositive from a diagram, reverse the two sides and negate <strong>both complete conditions</strong>. A condition already containing not loses that not when we negate it. Let\'s try all four patterns.</p>\\r\\n      <SignPatterns/>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-method\\"><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">The complete five-step routine</p><ol><li><strong>Find the indicator.</strong> Read its job in the whole sentence.</li><li><strong>Identify both complete ideas.</strong> Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.</li><li><strong>Place the roles.</strong> Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.</li><li><strong>Write the contrapositive.</strong> Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.</li><li><strong>Read both in English.</strong> Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.</li></ol></div>\\r\\n      <p>The original and its contrapositive describe the same relationship. If writing both helps you see that relationship, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn\'t copy it merely to have a second line.</p>\\r\\n      <p>Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition\'s absence and work back to the left condition\'s absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={10} label=\\"Practical diagramming questions\\" title=\\"Use writing to make your reasoning easier to check\\">\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-faq\\">\\r\\n        <details><summary>When should I diagram?</summary><p>While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the relationship on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How do I check whether my diagram is right?</summary><p>Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>What if writing takes time?</summary><p>Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the sentences you are actually working through. Your teacher can add timing to medium or advanced practice after the reasoning is stable.</p></details>\\r\\n        <details><summary>Should I always write the contrapositive?</summary><p>You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.</p></details>\\r\\n        <aside className=\\"cr-training-note\\"><h3>Why this practice asks for both</h3><p>In the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We\'re checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.</p></aside>\\r\\n        <details><summary>How should I abbreviate the conditions?</summary><p>Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. R might mean marked ready for pickup. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you\'re tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.</p></details>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?\\" options={[\'The labels need to be longer.\', \'The arrow makes the requirement enough.\', \'Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Longer labels would not repair the direction. The tray requires a paid stamp, but a stamped envelope can remain outside it.\', \'Only restricts tray envelopes to ones with paid stamps. In tray → Paid stamp preserves that requirement; the proposed reverse promises tray placement to every stamped envelope.\', \'Sentence order does not determine diagram order. Only identifies the paid stamp as required for tray placement.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>That error came from the language. Let\'s inspect precisely which idea an indicator refers to.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={11} label=\\"Find the target\\" title=\\"Read the whole idea the indicator refers to\\">\\r\\n      <p>An indicator\'s <strong>target</strong> is the complete word or phrase it refers to. In “All guests wearing blue badges receive vouchers,” all refers to guests wearing blue badges, not guests alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The target often comes after its indicator. But often isn\'t always. Try these sentences in order.</p>\\r\\n      {targetExamples.map(item => <TargetReader item={item} key={item.label}/>)}\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>“Editor approval is required for publication.”</p><p>“Publication requires editor approval.”</p><span>Same requirement, different placement</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Neither the closest noun nor the next word is a reliable target rule. Read which complete idea the sentence calls required. Then give that idea its role in the diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <p>When the wording still feels uncertain, we can test a proposed translation against an imagined case.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={12} label=\\"Check an interpretation\\" title=\\"Try the sentence from the inside\\">\\r\\n      <p>A <strong>Personalized If/Then</strong> lets you test what a proposed sufficient condition would establish. First read the ordinary meaning. Propose the sufficient idea. Imagine a case meeting it. Ask what the sentence now requires.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Only people wearing helmets ride the studio bikes.</blockquote><p>The sentence restricts riders to people wearing helmets. Keep that meaning while trying each proposed starting point.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-personalized\\"><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">First proposed starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m riding a studio bike, what does this sentence tell me?\\" options={[\'I am wearing a helmet.\', \'The helmet question is open.\']} correct={0} feedback={[\'The sentence puts every rider in the helmet-wearing group. Riding → Wearing helmet preserves that meaning.\', \'Only restricts who rides. If you meet the riding condition, the sentence does settle helmet wearing.\']}/></div><div><p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Try the other starting point</p><ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"If I\'m wearing a helmet, must I be riding a studio bike?\\" options={[\'Yes, the helmet proves riding.\', \'No, I could wear one without riding.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You gave every helmet wearer a ride. The sentence restricts riders; it does not make all helmet wearers riders.\', \'A helmet wearer waiting beside a bike fits the sentence. Wearing helmet → Riding would add a promise the original did not make.\']}/></div></div>\\r\\n      <p>The first reading matches the sentence. The second proposed arrow fails because it rules out a case the original permits. Use the imagined situation to inspect meaning, not to supply extra facts from what usually happens.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Use the What Test beside it</h3><p>Ask a sensible question about the expression: only what? People wearing helmets. What is required? Wearing a helmet. What is enough? Riding a studio bike, under this rule, establishes that requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The What Test finds the complete target. The Personalized If/Then checks what your proposed direction says about a case. Use them together when one part of the translation is unclear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We\'ve now read the relationship, understood its limits, written both directions and checked the language. Put those pieces together with fresh sentences.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={13} label=\\"Use the whole process\\" title=\\"Build an original and its contrapositive\\">\\r\\n      <p>For each sentence, identify the indicator and both complete conditions. Choose the original diagram, then its contrapositive. Read both back before checking.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You can look back. The job here is to produce and inspect the reasoning, not to prove you memorized every expression on the first reading. The labels below keep the complete ideas visible while you choose the directions.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-integrated-grid\\">{referencePracticeItems.map((item, index) => <IntegratedItem item={item} number={index + 1} key={item.sentence}/>)}</div>\\r\\n      <p>If an answer needs revision, name the exact issue: lost qualification, wrong role, reversed arrow or incomplete negation. Then retry that item. Your targeted Group 1 and Group 2 work gives you more practice with individual expressions; this set checks whether the steps hold together.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n    <section id=\\"reference-boundary\\" className=\\"cr-boundary co-section\\" data-reference-boundary data-toc data-toc-title=\\"Permission and instructions\\">\\r\\n      <p className=\\"co-kicker\\">Before the attached practice</p><h2>Keep what the sentence actually gives you</h2>\\r\\n      <p>The core process is now in place. One more distinction matters for the practice: a conditional can give permission or an instruction. Preserve that strength instead of turning it into a report of what someone did.</p>\\r\\n      <blockquote>Every guest with a ticket may use the cloakroom.</blockquote><p>May use gives permission here. A ticketed guest is allowed to use the cloakroom. The guest could keep their coat on and never go inside.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Lena is a guest with a ticket. Do we know that she used the cloakroom?\\" options={[\'Yes, the ticket establishes that she used it.\', \'No, we know she is allowed to use it.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned permission into a completed action. Lena meets the condition for permission, but we have no fact reporting her use of the cloakroom.\', \'The rule gives Lena permission because she is a guest with a ticket. Whether she used the cloakroom remains open.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Now compare an instruction. “If your coat is wet, hang it on the rack” tells you what to do with a wet coat. It does not report that someone followed the instruction.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"The notice says: If a tray is empty, return it to the counter. This tray is empty. What does the instruction establish?\\" options={[\'The tray has been returned.\', \'The instruction calls for returning the tray.\', \'The tray cannot be returned.\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You turned an instruction into a completed action. The notice has not reported that anyone returned this tray.\', \'The empty tray meets the condition in the instruction, so the notice calls for returning it. Whether someone returned it is a separate question.\', \'The instruction asks for a return. It does not say a return is impossible.\']}/>\\r\\n      <p>Allowed to use isn\'t used. Told to return isn\'t returned. Your conclusion should keep the meaning the sentence actually gave you.</p>\\r\\n    </section>\\r\\n  </div>;\\r\\n}\\r\\n"}'}]

Each fact gives a different starting point. Decide what it establishes before comparing the explanation.</p>\\r\\n      <FourCaseExplorer/>\\r\\n      <p>Starting from packing and concluding ready is the tempting <strong>reverse</strong>. Starting from not ready and concluding not packed is the tempting <strong>inverse</strong>. Neither follows from this rule alone.</p>\\r\\n      <p>The contrapositive starts from <strong>not packed</strong> and establishes <strong>not ready</strong>. Its reasoning depends on the missing requirement, not on reversing whichever words happen to appear.</p>\\r\\n      <p>We now understand the relationship in words. Symbols will let us record that understanding without carrying every word of the sentence.</p>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={8} label=\\"Build the first diagram\\" title=\\"Give the relationship a shorter form\\">\\r\\n      <p>Put the sufficient condition on the <strong>left</strong>, the necessary condition on the <strong>right</strong>, and a <strong>must arrow</strong> between them.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Sufficient: enough\\" right=\\"Necessary: required\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>The arrow means the left condition requires the right. It does not mean the left caused the right, or that the right happens later.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>Which idea goes on which side?</h3>\\r\\n      <p>The indicator helps you find a condition\'s job. Once you\'ve found that job, place the <strong>complete condition</strong> on its side of the arrow. We don\'t put the word if or only if in the diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-contrast\\"><div><h3>Left: sufficient</h3><p>This is the condition that\'s enough to establish the other one. Group 1 expressions such as <strong>if, when, all, every</strong> and <strong>any</strong> can identify this condition.</p><p>“All people who borrow a locker have a key.” All refers to <strong>people who borrow a locker</strong>. For someone in that group, having a key follows. Borrowing goes left.</p></div><div><h3>Right: necessary</h3><p>This is what the sufficient condition requires. Group 2 expressions such as <strong>only if, requires</strong> and <strong>must</strong> can identify the requirement.</p><p>“Borrowing a locker requires a key.” What is required? <strong>Having a key.</strong> That condition goes right.</p></div></div>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Borrows a locker\\" right=\\"Has a key\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Read it back: if someone borrows a locker, they have a key. The arrow records that connection. It hasn\'t told us that everyone with a key borrows a locker.</p>\\r\\n      <h3>The sentence can move. The roles stay the same.</h3>\\r\\n      <p>Compare these two versions of our order rule. The indicator is bold; the complete idea it refers to is in brackets.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>If</strong> [an order is marked ready for pickup], it has been packed.</p><p>An order has been packed <strong>if</strong> [it is marked ready for pickup].</p><span>If identifies the sufficient condition in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>In the second sentence, if appears in the middle. Its condition comes last, but being marked ready is still enough to establish packing. That condition still goes left. Don\'t let the first phrase in English choose the first side of your diagram.</p>\\r\\n      <RuleDiagram left=\\"Marked ready for pickup\\" right=\\"Has been packed\\"/>\\r\\n      <p>Now try the same move with a requirement.</p>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p><strong>Only</strong> [people with a key] borrow a locker.</p><p>A person borrows a locker <strong>only if</strong> [they have a key].</p><span>Only and only if identify the necessary condition here.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>Both sentences say locker borrowers have a key. In the first, only is at the beginning. In the second, only if is in the middle. Having a key is required in both, so it goes right. <strong>If</strong> and <strong>only if</strong> have different jobs; read only if as the whole expression.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"A person borrows a locker only if they have a key. Which condition goes on the right?\\" options={[\'Borrows a locker\', \'Has a key\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'Borrowing is what has the requirement. Ask what a locker borrower must have: a key. Borrowing goes left; having a key goes right.\', \'Having a key is required for borrowing. Only if appears in the middle, but it still identifies the necessary condition.\']}/>\\r\\n      <h3>The indicator can also follow its target</h3>\\r\\n      <div className=\\"cr-language-pair\\"><p>[Having a key] is <strong>required</strong> for borrowing a locker.</p><p>Borrowing a locker <strong>requires</strong> [having a key].</p><span>Having a key is the requirement in both sentences.</span></div>\\r\\n      <p>The target is the complete idea the indicator refers to. It comes before required in the first sentence and after requires in the second. Having a key still goes right. We find it by asking what is required, not by taking whichever words come next.</p>\\r\\n      <p>You\'ll work through the less familiar expressions in their Group 1 and Group 2 lessons. For each one, keep the whole sentence: what is enough, and what does it require?</p>\\r\\n      <p>Let\'s build an original diagram slowly. First find the indicator, then draw the arrow, place the complete idea it refers to, and fill the other side.</p>\\r\\n      <IncrementalWriter/>\\r\\n      <p>Now move the if-clause: “An order has been packed if it is marked ready for pickup.” The ready condition comes last in English but still goes left in the diagram. Its job hasn\'t changed.</p>\\r\\n      <ReferenceQuestion prompt=\\"Which diagram preserves the moved if-clause?\\" options={[\'Packed → Ready\', \'Ready → Packed\']} correct={1} feedback={[\'You let sentence order choose the arrow. The if-clause still makes being marked ready enough for packing.\', \'The sufficient condition is still marked ready. Where it appears in the sentence does not change what it establishes.\']}/>\\r\\n    </ReferenceStep>\\r\\n\\r\\n    <ReferenceStep number={9} label=\\"Write and read the contrapositive\\" title=\\"Write the meaning you already understand\\">\\r\\n      <p>Ready means the order is marked ready for pickup. Packed means it has been packed. Our original is Ready → Packed.</p>\\r\\n"}'}]

The rule leaves no room for exceptions.\\r\\n1: What relationship does an arrow represent in conditional notation like $A \\\\to B$? | It means if $A$ is true, then $B$ must be true. It expresses a logical guarantee, not cause or time order.\\r\\n2: Does stating the rule \\"All dogs are mammals\\" prove that any specific dog exists? | No. A rule states what must be true of any dog that exists, but it does not prove that its sufficient condition actually occurs.\\r\\n3: In the rule \\"All dogs are mammals,\\" how are the groups nested in a visual model? | The group of dogs fits entirely inside the group of mammals. Mammals can sit outside the dog group, but no dog can sit outside the mammal group.\\r\\n4: Given the rule \\"All volunteers wear badges\\" and the fact \\"Ari is a volunteer,\\" what is guaranteed about Ari? | Ari wears a badge ($\\\\text{Volunteer} \\\\to \\\\text{Wears badge}$). The rule applies to Ari because Ari meets the sufficient condition of being a volunteer.\\r\\n5: Why does the direction of a conditional arrow matter? | Switching the arrow changes the claim. A rule guarantees what follows from left to right, not the reverse.\\r\\n6: If the rule is $\\\\text{Dog} \\\\to \\\\text{Mammal}$ and Luna is a mammal, do you know Luna is a dog? | No. Other mammals like cats are allowed outside the dog group. Knowing the necessary condition does not guarantee the sufficient condition.\\r\\n7: Given \\"Every volunteer wears a badge,\\" Ari wears a badge. Does this establish Ari is a volunteer? | No. The rule requires volunteers to wear badges, but it does not say only volunteers wear them. Ari could be a staff member wearing a badge.\\r\\n8: How are conditional rules used in LSAT Must Be True questions? | You apply a conditional rule to an established fact to draw a guaranteed conclusion that follows the direction of the rule.\\r\\n9: What common flaw involves conditional logic on the LSAT? | A flaw occurs when an argument reverses a conditional rule, claiming more than the premises establish by assuming the necessary condition guarantees the sufficient condition.\\r\\n10: What is the role of conditional logic in LSAT Sufficient Assumption questions? | You must supply a missing rule that bridges the gap between the given evidence and the conclusion.\\r\\n11: Does the rule \\"All dogs are mammals\\" require the set of mammals to be larger than the set of dogs? | No. The rule permits mammals that are not dogs, but it does not require the sets to have different sizes.\\r\\n12: What is a sufficient condition? | A condition that is enough to guarantee that another condition occurs. It acts as the trigger for the rule.\\r\\n13: On which side of a conditional arrow does the sufficient condition belong? | On the left side ($\\\\text{Sufficient} \\\\to \\\\text{Necessary}$).\\r\\n14: If earning full credit is sufficient for passing an assignment, is full credit the only way to pass? | No. Sufficient means enough, not the only way. A student could earn less than full credit and still pass under a different pathway.\\r\\n15: Translate \\"If a person wins the race, that person receives a medal\\" and identify the sufficient condition. | $\\\\text{Wins} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives medal}$. Winning the race is the sufficient condition because it is enough to guarantee receiving a medal.\\r\\n16: What question should you ask yourself to identify if a condition is sufficient? | \\"If I have this, what must follow?\\"\\r\\n17: What is a necessary condition? | A condition that is required whenever the sufficient condition is met.\\r\\n18: On which side of a conditional arrow does the necessary condition belong? | On the right side ($\\\\text{Sufficient} \\\\to \\\\text{Necessary}$).\\r\\n19: Does meeting a necessary condition guarantee that the sufficient condition occurs? | No. Necessary means required, not enough. Knowing the necessary condition is met does not trigger the left side.\\r\\n20: Translate \\"Entering the archive requires a pass\\" and identify the necessary condition. | $\\\\text{Enters archive} \\\\to \\\\text{Has pass}$. Having a pass is necessary because it is required for entry.\\r\\n21: Under the rule $\\\\text{Full credit} \\\\to \\\\text{Passes}$, why is passing necessary for earning full credit? | Because you cannot earn full credit and fail. Passing is required whenever full credit is earned.\\r\\n22: What question should you ask yourself to identify if a condition is necessary? | \\"What must I have in order for the left side to be true?\\"\\r\\n23: How do you determine whether a condition is sufficient or necessary? | You read the specific rule in context. Conditions do not have fixed inherent roles; their roles depend on the rule being stated.\\r\\n24: What are the seven Group 1 indicators taught in the lesson? | If, when, where, all, the only, every, any.\\r\\n25: What side of the arrow does the target of a Group 1 indicator go on? | On the left side. Group 1 indicators introduce the sufficient condition.\\r\\n26: What key question must you ask when translating an indicator word? | \\"What is the indicator referring to?\\" You must identify the complete target idea.\\r\\n27: Translate and explain: \\"If a student revises the essay, the student receives feedback.\\" | $\\\\text{Revises essay} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives feedback}$. \\"If\\" refers to revising the essay, making it the sufficient condition on the left.\\r\\n28: Translate and explain: \\"A student receives feedback if the student revises the essay.\\" | $\\\\text{Revises essay} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives feedback}$. \\"If\\" still refers to revising the essay. Sentence word order changed, but the logical relationship did not.\\r\\n29: Translate and explain: \\"When the alarm sounds, the staff evacuate.\\" | $\\\\text{Alarm sounds} \\\\to \\\\text{Staff evacuate}$. \\"When\\" refers to the alarm sounding, making it sufficient for staff evacuation.\\r\\n30: Translate and explain: \\"Where there is smoke, there is fire.\\" | $\\\\text{Smoke} \\\\to \\\\text{Fire}$. \\"Where\\" refers to smoke, introducing the sufficient condition on the left.\\r\\n31: Translate and explain: \\"All registered guests receive a wristband.\\" | $\\\\text{Registered guest} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives wristband}$. \\"All\\" refers to registered guests, making registration sufficient for getting a wristband.\\r\\n32: Translate and explain: \\"Every volunteer wears a badge.\\" | $\\\\text{Volunteer} \\\\to \\\\text{Wears badge}$. \\"Every\\" refers to volunteers, making volunteer status sufficient to guarantee wearing a badge.\\r\\n33: Translate and explain: \\"Any applicant who submits late pays a fee.\\" | $\\\\text{Applicant submits late} \\\\to \\\\text{Pays fee}$. \\"Any\\" refers to applicants who submit late, making late submission sufficient for paying a fee.\\r\\n34: Why is it incorrect to translate \\"Any applicant who submits late pays a fee\\" as $\\\\text{Applicant} \\\\to \\\\text{Pays fee}$? | Because the rule does not apply to all applicants. It only applies to applicants who submit late, so the complete target idea must be kept on the left.\\r\\n35: Translate and explain: \\"The only people admitted are members.\\" | $\\\\text{Admitted} \\\\to \\\\text{Member}$. \\"The only\\" refers to people admitted, making being admitted the sufficient condition on the left.\\r\\n36: Translate and explain: \\"A report is reviewed if it is submitted before noon.\\" | $\\\\text{Submitted before noon} \\\\to \\\\text{Reviewed}$. \\"If\\" refers to submission before noon, so being submitted before noon guarantees review.\\r\\n37: Translate and explain: \\"The only vehicles permitted inside are electric vehicles.\\" | $\\\\text{Vehicle permitted inside} \\\\to \\\\text{Electric vehicle}$. \\"The only\\" refers to vehicles permitted inside, making permission to enter sufficient to establish it is electric.\\r\\n38: What are the seven Group 2 indicators taught in the lesson? | Only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.\\r\\n39: What side of the arrow does the target of a Group 2 indicator go on? | On the right side. Group 2 indicators introduce the necessary condition.\\r\\n40: Translate and explain: \\"A student enters the archive only if the student has a pass.\\" | $\\\\text{Enters archive} \\\\to \\\\text{Has pass}$. \\"Only if\\" refers to having a pass, making the pass a required (necessary) condition on the right.\\r\\n41: Compare the meaning of \\"A student enters if she has a pass\\" vs \\"A student enters only if she has a pass.\\" | \\"Enters if pass\\" ($\\\\text{Has pass} \\\\to \\\\text{Enters}$) makes the pass sufficient. \\"Enters only if pass\\" ($\\\\text{Enters} \\\\to \\\\text{Has pass}$) makes the pass required.\\r\\n42: Translate and explain: \\"Approval requires a signature.\\" | $\\\\text{Approved} \\\\to \\\\text{Has signature}$. \\"Requires\\" points to a signature as the necessary condition on the right.\\r\\n43: Translate and explain: \\"A permit is required for construction.\\" | $\\\\text{Construction} \\\\to \\\\text{Has permit}$. \\"Required\\" refers to a permit. The necessary condition appears first in the sentence, but still belongs on the right.\\r\\n44: Why must you avoid automatically putting words that physically follow an indicator onto a specific side of the arrow? | Because sentence structure varies. You must ask what complete idea the indicator refers to, identify whether it is sufficient or required, and verify the whole meaning.\\r\\n45: Compare \\"Only members are admitted\\" and \\"The only people admitted are members.\\" Do they yield the same rule? | Yes, both translate to $\\\\text{Admitted} \\\\to \\\\text{Member}$. \\"Only\\" targets members (required, right). \\"The only\\" targets people admitted (sufficient, left).\\r\\n46: Translate and explain: \\"Only registered guests receive wristbands.\\" | $\\\\text{Receives wristband} \\\\to \\\\text{Registered guest}$. \\"Only\\" refers to registered guests, making registration required (necessary) on the right.\\r\\n47: Translate and explain: \\"A finalist must attend the interview.\\" | $\\\\text{Finalist} \\\\to \\\\text{Attends interview}$. \\"Must\\" refers to attending the interview, making attendance a required condition on the right.\\r\\n48: Translate and explain: \\"A review occurs only when a complaint is filed.\\" | $\\\\text{Review occurs} \\\\to \\\\text{Complaint filed}$. \\"Only when\\" refers to filing a complaint, making the complaint a required condition on the right.\\r\\n49: Translate and explain: \\"The service operates only where a station is available.\\" | $\\\\text{Service operates} \\\\to \\\\text{Station available}$. \\"Only where\\" refers to station availability, making it a required condition on the right.\\r\\n50: Translate and explain: \\"A finalist always receives an invitation.\\" | $\\\\text{Finalist} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives invitation}$. \\"Always\\" indicates an exceptionless rule requiring that every finalist receives an invitation.\\r\\n51: Translate and explain: \\"A student graduates only if the student completes the project.\\" | $\\\\text{Graduates} \\\\to \\\\text{Completes project}$. \\"Only if\\" refers to completing the project, making project completion required on the right.\\r\\n52: Contrast how Group 1 indicators differ from Group 2 indicators in conditional translation. | Group 1 indicators (if, when, all, etc.) introduce the sufficient condition (left side). Group 2 indicators (only, required, must, etc.) introduce the necessary condition (right side).\\r\\n53: Given \\"A finalist must attend the interview,\\" Maya attended the interview. Does this prove Maya is a finalist? | No ($\\\\text{Finalist} \\\\to \\\\text{Attends interview}$). Attending the interview is required for finalists, but non-finalists might also be allowed to attend.\\r\\n54: Given \\"A finalist must attend the interview,\\" Maya is a finalist. What follows? | Maya must attend the interview ($\\\\text{Finalist} \\\\to \\\\text{Attends interview}$). Meeting the sufficient condition guarantees that the necessary condition is met.\\r\\n55: In the rule $\\\\text{Construction} \\\\to \\\\text{Has permit}$, does having a permit guarantee that construction will happen? | No. Having a permit is required for construction, but possessing a permit does not force someone to build.\\r\\n56: Are Group 1 and Group 2 indicators universal mechanical rules? | No. They are teaching guides. You must always read the full sentence context to verify what idea the indicator refers to and check the rule\'s overall meaning.\\r\\n57: Summarize the essential distinction between a sufficient condition and a necessary condition. | Sufficient is enough to trigger the outcome (goes left). Necessary is required whenever the outcome occurs (goes right).\\r\\n58: Why is it a logical flaw to infer $A$ from $B$ given the rule $A \\\\to B$? | Because $B$ is necessary for $A$, not sufficient. $B$ can occur without $A$ occurring.\\r\\n59: If full credit guarantees passing ($\\\\text{Full credit} \\\\to \\\\text{Passes}$), why can\'t we conclude someone earned full credit just because they passed? | Passing is necessary for full credit, but not sufficient. There may be other ways to pass without full credit.\\r\\n60: Rule: \\"All volunteers wear badges.\\" Fact: \\"Sam wears a badge.\\" What can we conclude about Sam? | Nothing definitive. Sam might be a volunteer, or Sam might be a staff member. The rule requires volunteers to wear badges, but non-volunteers may wear badges too.\\r\\n61: Rule: \\"Where there is smoke, there is fire.\\" Fact: \\"There is fire.\\" Does this guarantee there is smoke? | No ($\\\\text{Smoke} \\\\to \\\\text{Fire}$). Smoke guarantees fire, but fire does not guarantee smoke (a clean fire without smoke is permitted).\\r\\n62: Rule: \\"When the alarm sounds, the staff evacuate.\\" Fact: \\"Staff are evacuating.\\" Does this guarantee the alarm sounded? | No ($\\\\text{Alarm sounds} \\\\to \\\\text{Staff evacuate}$). The staff might be evacuating for a scheduled drill without an alarm sounding.\\r\\n63: How does adding \\"only\\" before \\"if\\" change the direction of the arrow? | \\"If\\" marks the left side (sufficient). Adding \\"only\\" turns it into \\"only if\\", which marks the right side (necessary).\\r\\n64: Translate \\"A complaint is filed only when a review occurs.\\" | $\\\\text{Complaint filed} \\\\to \\\\text{Review occurs}$. \\"Only when\\" refers to the review occurring, placing it on the right side as a necessary condition.\\r\\n65: Translate \\"The staff evacuate when the alarm sounds.\\" | $\\\\text{Alarm sounds} \\\\to \\\\text{Staff evacuate}$. \\"When\\" refers to the alarm sounding, placing it on the left side as a sufficient condition regardless of clause position.\\r\\n66: Translate and explain: \\"Crops grow only where water is abundant.\\" | $\\\\text{Crops grow} \\\\to \\\\text{Water abundant}$. \\"Only where\\" refers to abundant water, making it a necessary condition on the right.\\r\\n67: Translate and explain: \\"Where water is abundant, crops grow.\\" | $\\\\text{Water abundant} \\\\to \\\\text{Crops grow}$. \\"Where\\" refers to abundant water, making it a sufficient condition on the left.\\r\\n68: Translate \\"Every student who registers early receives a discount.\\" | $\\\\text{Student registers early} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives discount}$. \\"Every\\" refers to students who register early, not just any student.\\r\\n69: If a rule states $\\\\text{Submit before noon} \\\\to \\\\text{Reviewed}$, and a report is submitted at 2 PM, what does the rule tell us about the report? | Nothing. The rule only guarantees review for reports submitted before noon. It leaves late submissions open to being reviewed or not reviewed.\\r\\n70: What does the statement $\\\\text{Dog} \\\\to \\\\text{Mammal}$ permit regarding non-dogs? | It permits non-dogs to be mammals (like cats) or non-mammals (like lizards). It places no constraints on non-dogs.\\r\\n71: Compare \\"All members are admitted\\" and \\"Only members are admitted.\\" | \\"All members are admitted\\": $\\\\text{Member} \\\\to \\\\text{Admitted}$ (being a member guarantees admission). \\"Only members are admitted\\": $\\\\text{Admitted} \\\\to \\\\text{Member}$ (admission requires being a member).\\r\\n72: Identify the indicator and translate: \\"A fee is charged whenever a payment is late.\\" | Indicator: \\"Whenever\\" (functions like \\"when\\"/Group 1). Target: payment is late. Rule: $\\\\text{Payment late} \\to \\\\text{Fee charged}$.\\r\\n73: Translate \\"A fee is paid by any applicant who submits late.\\" | $\\\\text{Applicant submits late} \\\\to \\\\text{Pays fee}$. \\"Any\\" refers to applicants submitting late, placing that complete condition on the left regardless of sentence order.\\r\\n74: Translate \\"Graduation requires completing the project.\\" | $\\\\text{Graduates} \\\\to \\\\text{Completes project}$. \\"Requires\\" targets project completion, making it the necessary condition on the right.\\r\\n75: Premise 1: $\\\\text{Vehicle permitted inside} \\\\to \\\\text{Electric vehicle}$. Premise 2: Car X is permitted inside. What conclusion MUST be true? | Car X is an electric vehicle. Car X meets the sufficient condition, so the necessary condition guaranteed by the rule must follow.\\r\\n76: Premise: $\\\\text{Vehicle permitted inside} \\\\to \\\\text{Electric vehicle}$. Conclusion: Since Car Y is an electric vehicle, Car Y must be permitted inside. What is the logical flaw? | The argument reverses the conditional rule. Being an electric vehicle is required for permission, not sufficient to guarantee entry.\\r\\n77: Premise: Ari is a registered guest. Conclusion: Ari receives a wristband. What missing assumption guarantees this conclusion? | $\\\\text{Registered guest} \\\\to \\\\text{Receives wristband}$ (\\"All registered guests receive a wristband\\"). This connects the premise fact to the target conclusion.\\r\\n78: After translating a sentence into arrow notation, what final step should you take? | Check the meaning of your arrow against the original sentence to ensure the left side genuinely guarantees the right side and the right side is truly required.\\r\\n79: Why do \\"The only A are B\\" and \\"Only B are A\\" produce the same arrow $A \\\\to B$? | Because \\"The only\\" points to A as the sufficient condition, while \\"Only\\" points to B as the necessary condition. Both express that A cannot exist without B.\\r\\n2026-10-03-conditional-flashcards-downloaded.json 60\\r\\n0: What is a sufficient condition in conditional logic? | A condition that is enough on its own to guarantee an outcome (the trigger).\\r\\n1: What is a necessary condition in conditional logic? | A condition that is strictly required for an outcome to occur (the requirement).\\r\\n2: In the conditional statement \\"If $A$, then $B$\\", which variable represents the sufficient condition? | $A$ (the condition attached to \\"if\\" on the left side of the arrow).\\r\\n3: In the conditional statement \\"If $A$, then $B$\\", which variable represents the necessary condition? | $B$ (the outcome on the right side of the arrow).\\r\\n4: When visualizing a conditional statement with concentric circles, where is the sufficient condition placed? | In the smaller inner circle contained entirely within the larger necessary condition circle.\\r\\n5: What does the directional arrow in a conditional diagram ($A \\\\rightarrow B$) signify? | A one-way guarantee: if the left side ($A$) is true, the right side ($B$) must also be true.\\r\\n6: What are Group 1 (left-side) conditional indicator words? | Keywords that introduce a sufficient condition, such as *if*, *when*, *whenever*, *all*, *every*, *each*, and *any*.\\r\\n7: What are Group 2 (right-side) conditional indicator words? | Keywords that introduce a necessary condition, such as *only if*, *requires*, *must*, *prerequisite*, and *only*.\\r\\n8: How does the conditional indicator \\"only if\\" differ from \\"if\\"? | \\"If\\" introduces a sufficient condition (left side), whereas \\"only if\\" introduces a necessary condition (right side).\\r\\n9: What valid inference can be made when you learn that a sufficient condition is met? | You can conclude that the necessary condition must also be true.\\r\\n10: What valid inference can be made when you learn that a necessary condition is NOT met? | You can conclude that the sufficient condition is also not true (the contrapositive).\\r\\n11: What is a contrapositive? | A logically equivalent restatement of a conditional that flips the order and negates both conditions ($\\\\text{not } B \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } A$).\\r\\n12: What invalid inference occurs when you infer the left side is true because the right side is true? | Reversing the conditional (affirming the necessary condition).\\r\\n13: What invalid inference occurs when you infer the right side is false because the left side is false? | Inverting the conditional (denying the sufficient condition).\\r\\n14: What logical flaw is committed when an argument treats a necessary condition as if it were sufficient? | Confusing necessary and sufficient conditions.\\r\\n15: Does learning that a sufficient condition is false allow you to conclude that the necessary condition is false? | No; when the trigger is false, the rule simply disappears and gives no information about the necessary condition.\\r\\n16: How do you diagram statements quantified by \\"No\\" (e.g., \\"No $A$s are $B$s\\")? | Place the subject on the left and negate the second term on the right ($A \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } B$).\\r\\n17: What is the \\"if not\\" translation method for the word \\"unless\\"? | Replace \\"unless\\" with \\"if not\\" and place the attached idea, negated, on the left side of the arrow.\\r\\n18: What is the \\"requirement\\" translation method for the word \\"unless\\"? | Place the idea following \\"unless\\" directly on the right side as a necessary condition, and negate the remaining idea on the left.\\r\\n19: Which keywords follow the exact same translation rules as \\"unless\\"? | *Until* and *without*.\\r\\n20: How does the word \\"and\\" transform when taking the contrapositive of a conditional rule? | \\"And\\" transforms into \\"or\\".\\r\\n21: How does the word \\"or\\" transform when taking the contrapositive of a conditional rule? | \\"Or\\" transforms into \\"and\\".\\r\\n22: What is the contrapositive of $A \\\\text{ and } B \\\\rightarrow C$? | $\\\\text{not } C \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } A \\\\text{ or } \\\\text{not } B$.\\r\\n23: What is the contrapositive of $X \\\\rightarrow Y \\\\text{ or } \\\\text{not } Z$? | $\\\\text{not } Y \\\\text{ and } Z \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } X$.\\r\\n24: What is a biconditional statement? | A conditional relationship that flows in both directions, indicated by phrases like \\"if and only if\\" ($A \\\\leftrightarrow B$).\\r\\n25: What two scenarios are possible under the biconditional statement $A \\\\leftrightarrow B$? | 1. Both $A$ and $B$ occur.\\r\\n2. Neither $A$ nor $B$ occurs.\\r\\n26: How can the word \\"otherwise\\" create a biconditional statement? | By providing a second condition that is the explicit logical opposite of the first rule.\\r\\n27: What structural condition is required to chain two conditional statements together? | The necessary condition of one statement must match the sufficient condition of the other.\\r\\n28: Given the chain $A \\\\rightarrow B \\\\rightarrow C \\\\rightarrow D$, what main conclusion can be inferred? | $A \\\\rightarrow D$ (and its contrapositive $\\\\text{not } D \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } A$).\\r\\n29: What is the subset visual model for understanding conditionals? | Visualizing the sufficient condition as a smaller subset category within a broader necessary condition category.\\r\\n30: What is the partial list of requirements model for understanding conditionals? | Visualizing the necessary condition as one known ingredient required for a goal, alongside potentially other unknown requirements.\\r\\n31: What is the \\"one of many paths\\" model for understanding conditionals? | Visualizing the sufficient condition as one specific path that leads to a destination (necessary condition), among other possible paths.\\r\\n32: How does LSAT interpretation of a conditional statement differ from real-life interpretation? | The LSAT requires strict, literal interpretation without relying on conversational assumptions or unstated real-world context.\\r\\n33: Diagram the statement: \\"Anyone in New York is in the USA.\\" | $\\\\text{New York} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{USA}$.\\r\\n34: Diagram the statement: \\"You must study hard if you want to get a great LSAT score.\\" | $\\\\text{Great LSAT Score} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Study Hard}$.\\r\\n35: Diagram the statement: \\"Finishing in the top three guarantees winning a medal.\\" | $\\\\text{Top Three} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Medal}$.\\r\\n36: Does the statement \\"Finishing in the top three guarantees winning a medal\\" mean that competitors outside the top three do not get medals? | No; top three guarantees a medal, but the statement leaves open the possibility that others might also receive medals.\\r\\n37: Diagram the statement: \\"Eddie can\'t go to Legoland until he feeds his rabbit.\\" | $\\\\text{Legoland} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Feed Rabbit}$ (or $\\\\text{Not Feed Rabbit} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Not Legoland}$).\\r\\n38: Diagram the statement: \\"No camels attend law school.\\" | $\\\\text{Camel} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Not Law School}$ (or $\\\\text{Law School} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Not Camel}$).\\r\\n39: Diagram the statement: \\"Happiness is never achieved unless love or passion is present.\\" | $\\\\text{Happiness} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Love or Passion}$ (or $\\\\text{Not Love and Not Passion} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Not Happiness}$).\\r\\n40: How do you translate the negative quantifier \\"Few $A$s are $B$\\" into a positive translation? | Most $A$s are not $B$.\\r\\n41: How do you translate the negative quantifier \\"Not all $A$s are $B$\\" into a positive translation? | Some $A$s are not $B$.\\r\\n42: Given the rule $A \\\\rightarrow B \\\\text{ or } C$, and the facts that $A$ is true and $C$ is false, what fact can be inferred? | $B$ must be true.\\r\\n43: Given the rules $A \\\\text{ and } B \\\\rightarrow C$, and $C \\\\rightarrow D$, alongside the fact that $A$ is true, what additional fact is needed to guarantee $D$? | $B$ must be true.\\r\\n44: What type of condition does the word \\"prerequisite\\" introduce? | A necessary condition (Group 2 word, placed on the right side of the arrow).\\r\\n45: Diagram the statement: \\"Running requires that you\'ve learned to walk.\\" | $\\\\text{Run} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Learn to Walk}$.\\r\\n46: Diagram the statement: \\"Enjoying fudge implies enjoying sugar.\\" | $\\\\text{Enjoy Fudge} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Enjoy Sugar}$.\\r\\n47: Diagram the statement: \\"Lou\'s golf balls are each covered in bird poop.\\" | $\\\\text{Lou\'s Golf Ball} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Bird Poop}$.\\r\\n48: Diagram the statement: \\"Real men never shy away from a yogurt parfait.\\" | $\\\\text{Real Man} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Not Shy Away}$.\\r\\n49: Diagram the statement: \\"Using breath strips instead of mints is done only by cool people.\\" | $\\\\text{Breath Strips} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Cool Person}$.\\r\\n50: Diagram the statement: \\"Vanessa can\'t work without tea and snacks.\\" | $\\\\text{Work} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Tea and Snacks}$ (or $\\\\text{Not Tea or Not Snacks} \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{Not Work}$).\\r\\n51: Why is standard conditional diagramming supplemented with mental visual models? | Standard arrows do not visually explain why moving backward or negating both sides without flipping is invalid.\\r\\n52: When is explicit conditional diagramming most useful during the LSAT? | When handling complex conditional chains, tricky indicator language (e.g., *unless*), or parallel reasoning questions.\\r\\n53: Is a conditional statement logically equivalent to its contrapositive? | Yes; a conditional rule and its contrapositive express the exact same logical meaning.\\r\\n54: What happens to a multi-step conditional chain ($A \\\\rightarrow B \\\\rightarrow C$) when taking its contrapositive? | The entire chain is reversed and every term is negated: $\\\\text{not } C \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } B \\\\rightarrow \\\\text{not } A$.\\r\\n55: What is the primary function of Group 1 conditional keywords? | To signal that the attached concept acts as the trigger or sufficient condition.\\r\\n56: What is the primary function of Group 2 conditional keywords? | To signal that the attached concept acts as a requirement or necessary condition.\\r\\n57: If performing an action out of self-interest means it should NOT be considered generous, does an action performed without self-interest prove it IS generous? | No; concluding that it is generous commits the invalid flaw of inverting the conditional.\\r\\n58: In the statement \\"A sidewalk is open to the public only if it has no ice,\\" which condition is necessary? | Having no ice on the sidewalk.\\r\\n59: Diagram the statement: \\"Jeff takes a vacation if, but only if, Trixie gets a promotion.\\" | $\\\\text{Vacation} \\\\leftrightarrow \\\\text{Promotion}$.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"5b8309","wall_time_seconds":0.177198,"exit_code":1,"original_token_count":81,"output":"rg: brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/ASSET-MANIFEST.md: The system cannot find the file specified.
